\displaystyle=\E_{x\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(\hat{\sigma}_{k,d}\E_{u\sim\rho}[\overline{P}_{k,d}(\left<u,x\right>)]-\hat{h}_{k,d}\overline{P}_{k,d}(\left<x,e_{1}\right>)\Big)\Big)^{2}.

(B.7)

When the neural network
\rho
is rotationally invariant, we can simplify the above equation by invoking
Lemma

A.2
, which states that

\displaystyle\E_{u\sim\rho}[\overline{P}_{k,d}(\left<u,x\right>)]=\E_{u\sim\rho}[P_{k,d}(\left<e_{1},u\right>)]\overline{P}_{k,d}(\left<e_{1},x\right>).

(B.8)

Continuing
Equation

B.4
above we have,

\displaystyle 2L(\rho)

\displaystyle=\E_{x\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\hat{\sigma}_{k,d}\E_{u\sim\rho}[P_{k,d}(\left<e_{1},u\right>)]\overline{P}_{k,d}(\left<e_{1},x\right>)-\hat{h}_{k,d}\overline{P}_{k,d}(\left<e_{1},x\right>)\Big)^{2}

(B.9)

\displaystyle=\E_{x\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\hat{\sigma}_{k,d}\E_{u\sim\rho}[P_{k,d}(\left<e_{1},u\right>)]-\hat{h}_{k,d})\overline{P}_{k,d}(\left<e_{1},x\right>)\Big)^{2}

(B.10)

Now we expand the square by invoking
Lemma

A.5
. In particular,
Lemma

A.5
states that

\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}[\overline{P}_{k,d}(\left<e_{1},x\right>)\overline{P}_{k^{\prime},d}(\left<e_{1},x\right>)]=\mathbf{1}\left[k=k^{\prime}\right]P_{k,d}(\left<e_{1},e_{1}\right>)=\mathbf{1}\left[k=k^{\prime}\right].

(B.11)

Consequently,

\displaystyle 2L(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(\hat{\sigma}_{k,d}\E_{u\sim\rho}[P_{k,d}(\left<e_{1},u\right>)]-\hat{h}_{k,d}\Big)^{2},

(B.12)

as desired.

To prove the second part of this lemma, in the following, we assume that (1)
\hat{h}_{k,d}=0,\forall k\not\in\{0,2,4\}
, (2)
\hat{h}_{0,d}=\hat{\sigma}_{0,d}
, and (3) that
\sigma
is a degree-4 polynomial. Then we get

\displaystyle L(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}}{2}\Big(\E_{u\sim\rho}[P_{2,d}(w)]-\gamma_{2}\Big)^{2}+\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}}{2}\Big(\E_{u\sim\rho}[P_{4,d}(w)]-\gamma_{4}\Big)^{2}+\sum_{k\in\{1,3\}}\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{k,d}^{2}}{2}\left(\E_{u\sim\rho}[P_{k,d}(w)]\right)^{2}.

(B.13)

Since
\rho
is symmetric and
P_{k,d}(w)
is an odd function when
k
is odd, we get

\displaystyle\forall k\in\{1,3\},\quad\E_{u\sim\rho}[P_{k,d}(w)]=0.

(B.14)

It follows directly that

\displaystyle L(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}}{2}\Big(\E_{u\sim\rho}[P_{2,d}(w)]-\gamma_{2}\Big)^{2}+\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}}{2}\Big(\E_{u\sim\rho}[P_{4,d}(w)]-\gamma_{4}\Big)^{2}

(B.15)

∎

B.2

Proof of
Lemma

4.3
and
Lemma

4.4

The goal of this subsection is to show
Lemma

4.3
, which states that
\rho_{t}
remains symmetric and rotationally invariant under the infinite-width population dynamics, and to show
Lemma

4.4
which describes the dynamics of the first coordinates of the particles in
\rho_{t}
.

The following lemma is a first step. Recall that when
\rho
is rotationally invariant, then
Lemma

4.2
allows us to rewrite
L(\rho)=\frac{1}{2}\E_{x\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Big(f_{\rho}(x)-y\Big)^{2}
as
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(\hat{\sigma}_{k,d}\E_{u\sim\rho}[{P_{k,d}}(w)]-\hat{h}_{k,d}\Big)^{2}
. The following lemma states that the gradient from the two formulas for
L(\rho)
are the same when
\rho
is rotationally invariant.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\rho
be a distribution on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and let
\nu
be a distribution on
[-1,1]
. Define

\displaystyle F(\nu)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(\hat{\sigma}_{k,d}\E_{w\sim\nu}[P_{k,d}(w)]-\hat{h}_{k,d}\Big)^{2}\,.

(B.16)

If
\rho
is rotationally invariant as in
Definition

4.1
and
\nu
is the marginal distribution of
w
for
u=(w,z)\sim\rho
, then for any particle
u=(w,z)
, we have

\displaystyle(1-w^{2})\nabla_{w}F(\nu)={\textup{grad}}_{w}L(\rho)

(B.17)

where
{\textup{grad}}_{w}L(\rho)
denotes the first coordinate of
{\textup{grad}}_{u}L(\rho)
.

Proof of
Lemma

B.1
.

For any particle
u=(w,z)
, we write the first coordinate of
\nabla_{u}L(\rho)
as
\nabla_{w}L(\rho)
, and the vector consisting of the last
(d-1)
coordinates as
\nabla_{z}L(\rho)
. Observe that

\displaystyle{\textup{grad}}_{w}L(\rho)

\displaystyle=\left<e_{1},{\textup{grad}}_{u}L(\rho)\right>

(B.18)

\displaystyle=\left<e_{1},(I-uu^{\top})\nabla_{u}L(\rho)\right>

(B.19)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{w}L(\rho)-w\left<u,\nabla_{u}L(\rho)\right>

(B.20)

\displaystyle=(1-w^{2})\nabla_{w}L(\rho)-w\left<z,\nabla_{z}L(\rho)\right>

(B.21)

On the other hand, suppose
\rho
satisfies the rotational invariance property, and that
\nu
is the marginal distribution of the first coordinate under
\rho
. Then, we can write
\rho
in terms of
\nu
— for any
w\in[-1,1]
, the distribution of
z
conditioned on
w
is
\sqrt{1-w^{2}}\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Thus, since
L(\rho)=F(\nu)
by
Lemma

4.2
, we have by the chain rule that

\displaystyle\nabla_{w}F(\nu)

\displaystyle=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\\
u=we_{1}+\sqrt{1-w^{2}}\xi\end{subarray}}\Big[\Big\langle\nabla_{u}L(\rho),\frac{\partial u}{\partial w}\Big\rangle\Big]

(B.22)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{w}L(\rho)+\E_{\xi\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-2}}\Big[\Big\langle\nabla_{z}L(\rho)\Big|_{z=\sqrt{1-w^{2}}\xi},\frac{\partial z}{\partial w}\Big\rangle\Big]

(B.23)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{w}L(\rho)+\E_{\xi\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-2}}\Big[\Big\langle\nabla_{z}L(\rho)\Big|_{z=\sqrt{1-w^{2}}\xi},-\frac{w}{\sqrt{1-w^{2}}}\xi\Big\rangle\Big]

(B.24)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{w}L(\rho)+\E_{\xi\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-2}}\Big[\Big\langle\nabla_{z}L(\rho)\Big|_{z=\sqrt{1-w^{2}}\xi},-\frac{w}{1-w^{2}}z\Big\rangle\Big]

(B.25)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{w}L(\rho)-\frac{w}{1-w^{2}}\E_{\xi\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-2}}[\langle\nabla_{z}L(\rho),z\rangle]

(B.26)

Moreover, for any fixed
w\in[-1,1]
, the quantity
\langle\nabla_{z}L(\rho),z\rangle
does not depend on
z
, since by
Lemma

B.2
, for any rotation matrix
A
and
z^{\prime}=Az
, we have
\langle\nabla_{z^{\prime}}L(\rho),z^{\prime}\rangle=\langle A\cdot\nabla_{z}L(\rho),Az\rangle=\langle\nabla_{z}L(\rho),z\rangle
. Thus, for any
w\in[-1,1]
and
z\in\sqrt{1-w^{2}}\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
, we can write

\displaystyle(1-w^{2})\nabla_{w}F(\nu)=(1-w^{2})\nabla_{w}L(\rho)-w\left<z,\nabla_{z}L(\rho)\right>={\textup{grad}}_{w}L(\rho)

(B.27)

as desired.
∎

In the proof of the above lemma, we used the following fact which we now prove: when
\rho
is rotationally invariant,
\nabla_{u}L(\rho)
is rotationally equivariant as a function of
u
, i.e. if
u
is rotated by a rotation matrix
A
which only modifies the last
(d-1)
coordinates, then
\nabla_{u}L(\rho)
is rotated by
A
as well.

Lemma B.2

(Gradient is Rotationally Equivariant)
.

Let
\rho
be a rotationally invariant distribution as in
Definition

4.1
. Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a rotation matrix with
Ae_{1}=e_{1}
, i.e.
A
only modifies the last
(d-1)
coordinates. Let
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
u^{\prime}=Au
. Then,
\nabla_{u^{\prime}}L(\rho)=A\cdot\nabla_{u}L(\rho)
, and
{\textup{grad}}_{u^{\prime}}L(\rho)=A\cdot{\textup{grad}}_{u}L(\rho)
.

Proof.

The proof is by a straightforward calculation:

\displaystyle\nabla_{u^{\prime}}L(\rho)

\displaystyle=\E_{x\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}[(f_{\rho}(x)-y(x))\sigma^{\prime}(\left<u^{\prime},x\right>)x]

(B.28)

\displaystyle=\E_{x\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}[(f_{\rho}(x)-h(\left<e_{1},x\right>))\sigma^{\prime}(\left<u^{\prime},x\right>)x]

(B.29)

\displaystyle=\E_{x\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}[(f_{\rho}(Ax)-h(\left<e_{1},Ax\right>))\sigma^{\prime}(\left<u^{\prime},Ax\right>)Ax]

(By rotational invariance of uniform distribution on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
)

\displaystyle=A\E_{x\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}[(f_{\rho}(Ax)-h(\left<e_{1},Ax\right>))\sigma^{\prime}(\left<u^{\prime},Ax\right>)x]

(B.30)

\displaystyle=A\E_{x\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}[(f_{\rho}(Ax)-h(\left<e_{1},Ax\right>))\sigma^{\prime}(\left<Au,Ax\right>)x]

(By definition of
u^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle=A\E_{x\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}[(f_{\rho}(Ax)-h(\left<e_{1},Ax\right>))\sigma^{\prime}(\left<u,x\right>)x]

(B.c.
A
is a rotation matrix so
A^{\top}A={I_{d\times d}}
)

\displaystyle=A\E_{x\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}[(f_{\rho}(x)-h(\left<e_{1},Ax\right>))\sigma^{\prime}(\left<u,x\right>)x]

(B.c.
f_{\rho}(Ax)=f_{\rho}(x)
by rotational invariance of
\rho
)

\displaystyle=A\E_{x\sim\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}[(f_{\rho}(x)-h(\left<e_{1},x\right>))\sigma^{\prime}(\left<u,x\right>)x]

(B.c.
Ae_{1}=e_{1}
and
A^{\top}A={I_{d\times d}}
)

\displaystyle=A\cdot\nabla_{u}L(\rho)

(B.31)

This proves the first statement of the lemma. The second statement also follows from a similar calculation:

\displaystyle{\textup{grad}}_{u^{\prime}}L(\rho)

\displaystyle=(I-u^{\prime}(u^{\prime})^{\top})\cdot\nabla_{u^{\prime}}L(\rho)

(B.32)

\displaystyle=(I-Auu^{\top}A^{\top})\cdot A\cdot\nabla_{u}L(\rho)

(B.33)

\displaystyle=A\cdot\nabla_{u}L(\rho)-Auu^{\top}A^{\top}A\cdot\nabla_{u}L(\rho)

(B.34)

\displaystyle=A\cdot\nabla_{u}L(\rho)-Auu^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{u}L(\rho)

(B.c.
A^{\top}A={I_{d\times d}}
)

\displaystyle=A\cdot(I-uu^{\top})\cdot\nabla_{u}L(\rho)

(B.35)

\displaystyle=A\cdot{\textup{grad}}_{u}L(\rho)

(B.36)

as desired.
∎

As a corollary, we obtain our first formula for the dynamics of the 1-dimensional particles:

Lemma B.3

(One-Dimensional Velocity)
.

Suppose we are in the setting of
Theorem

3.3
and that
\rho_{t}
is rotationally invariant. Then, for any particle
u_{t}
(where we omit the initialization
\chi
for convenience), if
w_{t}
is the first coordinate of
u_{t}
, then

\displaystyle\frac{dw_{t}}{dt}

\displaystyle=-(1-w_{t}^{2})\sum_{k=0}^{4}\Big(\hat{\sigma}_{k,d}\E_{u\sim\rho_{t}}[{P_{k,d}}(w)]-\hat{h}_{k,d}\Big)\cdot\hat{\sigma}_{k,d}{P_{k,d}}^{\prime}(w)\,.

(B.37)

Proof of
Lemma

B.3
.

This follows directly from
Lemma

B.1
, and because the activation
\sigma
and the target
h
only have nonzero coefficients
\hat{\sigma}_{k,d}
and
\hat{h}_{k,d}
for
k=0,\ldots,4
.
∎

We now complete the proof of
Lemma

4.3
, i.e. that
\rho_{t}
remains rotationally invariant and symmetric if it is defined according to the population projected gradient flow as in
Equation

3.6
and
Equation

3.7
. To prove that
\rho_{t}
remains symmetric, we make use of the above formula for the 1-dimensional dynamics.

Proof of
Lemma

4.3
.

First, we show the rotational invariance property. Assume inductively that at a time
t
,
\rho_{t}
satisfies the desired rotational invariance property — it holds at initialization since
\rho_{0}
is the uniform distribution on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Consider a particle
u_{t}
and let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a rotation matrix which only modifies the last
(d-1)
coordinates. Let
u^{\prime}_{t}=Au_{t}
be another particle. Then, it follows from
Lemma

B.2
that

\displaystyle\frac{du_{t}^{\prime}}{dt}

\displaystyle=A\frac{du_{t}}{dt}

(B.38)

Thus, for a fixed
w\in[-1,1]
, if
z\sim\sqrt{1-w^{2}}\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
, then the distribution of
\frac{dz}{dt}
is uniform on
c\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
for a fixed scalar
c
that depends on
w
. Therefore,
\rho
will remain rotationally invariant.

Next, we prove that
\rho_{t}
is symmetric. First observe that
\rho_{t}
is symmetric at initialization, since it is the uniform distribution on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Now, suppose that
\rho_{t}
is symmetric at time
t
. The polynomial
{P_{k,d}}
is odd if
k
is odd and even if
k
is even — this can be seen from induction on
Equation

A.2
and
Equation

A.3
. Thus,
\E_{u\sim\rho_{t}}[{P_{k,d}}(w)]=0
for odd
k
by our assumption that
\rho_{t}
is symmetric. Additionally, recall that we defined
h
so that
\hat{\sigma}_{0,d}=\hat{h}_{0,d}
and
\hat{h}_{k,d}=0
for odd
k
. Thus, by our definition of
D_{2,t}
and
D_{4,t}
in
Section

4
,

\displaystyle\frac{dw_{t}}{dt}

\displaystyle=-(1-w_{t}^{2})(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}{P_{2,d}}^{\prime}(w_{t})+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}{P_{4,d}}^{\prime}(w_{t}))

(B.39)

by
Lemma

B.3
. Since
{P_{2,d}}
and
{P_{4,d}}
are even polynomials, we know
{P_{2,d}}^{\prime}
and
{P_{4,d}}^{\prime}
are odd polynomials, and thus
\frac{dw_{t}}{dt}
is an odd polynomial in
w_{t}
. In other words, if
w_{t}
and
w_{t}^{\prime}
are the first coordinates of two particles
u_{t}
and
u_{t}^{\prime}
respectively, such that
w_{t}^{\prime}=-w_{t}
, then
\frac{dw_{t}^{\prime}}{dt}=-\frac{dw_{t}}{dt}
. Thus, the distribution of
\frac{dw_{t}}{dt}
is symmetric, meaning that
\rho_{t}
will remain symmetric.
∎

In the rest of the proofs for the infinite-width population dynamics, we make use of the symmetry property. In particular, many of the lemmas are stated for
w>0
or
\iota>0
— however, the generalizations to
w<0
or
\iota<0
also hold. We use this symmetry implicitly throughout the proofs in this section.

In
Lemma

4.4
, we are simply rewriting our formula for the 1-dimensional dynamics in a more convenient form. The following proof shows the detailed calculation.

Proof of
Lemma

4.4
.

By
Lemma

B.3
and the fact that only the second and fourth order terms are nonzero (which follows from
Lemma

4.3
, the fact that
{P_{k,d}}
is an odd polynomial for odd
k
(
Equation

A.2
and
Equation

A.3
) and because
\hat{h}_{1,d}=\hat{h}_{3,d}=0
), the velocity of
w
is equal to the following:

\displaystyle{\textup{grad}}_{w}L(\rho)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})\cdot\Big(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2}(t){P_{2,d}}^{\prime}(w)+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4}(t){P_{4,d}}^{\prime}(w)\Big)

(B.40)

where we use
{\textup{grad}}_{w}L(\rho)
to denote the first coordinate of
{\textup{grad}}_{u}L(\rho)
. For convenience, during the rest of the proof of this lemma, let
{\eta_{d}}^{(1)}=\frac{d}{d-1}
,
{\eta_{d}}^{(2)}=\frac{d^{2}+6d+8}{d^{2}-1}
and
{\eta_{d}}^{(3)}=\frac{6d+12}{d^{2}-1}
. Then, by
Equation

A.4
,

\displaystyle{\textup{grad}}_{w}L(\rho)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})\cdot\Big(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2}(t)P_{2,d}^{\prime}(w)+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4}(t)P_{4,d}^{\prime}(w)\Big)

(B.41)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})\cdot\Big(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2}(t)\cdot 2{\eta_{d}}^{(1)}w+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4}(t)\cdot(4{\eta_{d}}^{(2)}w^{3}-2{\eta_{d}}^{(3)}w)\Big)

(B.42)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})\cdot\Big(P_{t}(w)+2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2}(t)\cdot({\eta_{d}}^{(1)}-1)w

(B.43)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4}(t)\cdot({\eta_{d}}^{(2)}-1)w^{3}-2\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4}(t){\eta_{d}}^{(3)}w\Big)

(B.44)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})\cdot\Big(P_{t}(w)+Q_{t}(w)\Big)

(B.45)

Here we have chosen
{\lambda_{d}}^{(1)}=2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}({\eta_{d}}^{(1)}-1)D_{2}(t)-2\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4}(t){\eta_{d}}^{(3)}
and
{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}=4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4}(t)({\eta_{d}}^{(2)}-1)
. Observe that
|{\lambda_{d}}^{(1)}|,|{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}|\leq O(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|}{d})
, as desired.
∎

B.3

Analysis of Phase 1

Recall the formal definition of Phase 1 in
Definition

4.5
. Intuitively, during Phase 1, the particles are all growing at a similar rate — because the particles
w
are all less than
{w_{\textup{max}}}=\frac{1}{\log d}
in magnitude, the cubic term involving
w^{3}
in the velocity of
w
does not have a significant effect on the growth of
w
. We formalize this in the following lemma.

Lemma B.4

(Uniform Growth Lemma)
.

Suppose we are in the setting of
Theorem

3.3
. Consider a time interval
[t_{0},t_{1}]\in[0,T_{2}]
in Phase 1 or 2. We are interested in quantifying the (relative) growth rate of the neurons indexed (or initialized) at
\iota_{1}
and
\iota_{2}
(where
0<\iota_{1}<\iota_{2}
), defined as

\displaystyle F_{1}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{w_{t_{1}}(\iota_{1})}{w_{t_{0}}(\iota_{1})}

\displaystyle F_{2}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{w_{t_{1}}(\iota_{2})}{w_{t_{0}}(\iota_{2})}

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
such that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\lesssim\delta\leq\frac{1}{\log F_{2}}
— here, we implicitly assume that
F_{2}\leq e^{d}
. We also assume that
\delta\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}
. Further assume that
w_{t_{1}}(\iota_{2})=\delta
. Finally assume that
\mathbb{P}(|\iota|\geq|\iota_{2}|)\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}
. Then,

\displaystyle F_{1}\asymp F_{2}

(B.46)

and additionally,

\displaystyle F_{1}\asymp\exp\Big(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|dt\Big)

(B.47)

Finally,

\displaystyle t_{1}-t_{0}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}\gamma_{2}}\log F_{2}

(B.48)

We note that
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are both at least
1
.

Note that this lemma also holds for
\iota_{2}<\iota_{1}<0
, and with
w_{t_{1}}(\iota_{2})=-\delta
, by the symmetry of
\rho_{t}
.

Proof of
Lemma

B.4
.

In this proof, we define
\tau=\sqrt{\gamma_{2}}
— then, by
3.2
, we can write
\gamma_{4}=\beta\cdot\tau^{4}
where
1.1\leq\beta
and
\beta
is less than some universal constant
c_{1}
. Suppose at time
t_{1}
,
w_{t_{1}}(\iota_{2})=\delta
. One useful observation, which we use in the rest of this proof, is that for all
t\leq t_{1}
,
w_{t}(\iota_{2})\leq\delta
, since for
w\gtrsim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}
, we have
\frac{dw}{dt}\geq 0
by
Lemma

4.4
(here we used the assumption that
\delta\gtrsim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}
). Thus,

\displaystyle\E_{w\sim\rho}[{P_{2,d}}(w)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(|\iota|>|\iota_{2}|)\cdot\E_{w\sim\rho}[{P_{2,d}}(w)\mid|\iota|>|\iota_{2}|]+\mathbb{P}(|\iota|<|\iota_{2}|)\cdot\E_{w\sim\rho}[{P_{2,d}}(w)\mid|\iota|<|\iota_{2}|]

(B.49)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}+\E_{w\sim\rho}[{P_{2,d}}(w)\mid|\iota|<|\iota_{2}|]

(B.c.
|{P_{2,d}}(w)|\leq 1
by Eq. (2.116) of
Atkinson and Han 2012
and
\mathbb{P}(|\iota|>|\iota_{2}|)\leq\delta
)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}+\delta^{2}+O\Big(\frac{1}{d}\Big)

(By
Equation

A.4
and b.c.
|w_{t}(\iota)|\leq\delta
if
|\iota|\leq|\iota_{2}|
by
Proposition

B.33
)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\tau^{4}}{8}+O\Big(\frac{1}{d}\Big)

(B.c.
\delta\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}
and
\gamma_{2}=\tau^{2}
)

Thus, writing
\gamma_{2}=\tau^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle|{D_{2,t}}|=|\E_{w\sim\rho}[{P_{2,d}}(w)]-\tau^{2}|\geq\Big|\frac{\tau^{4}}{8}+O\Big(\frac{1}{d}\Big)-\tau^{2}\Big|\geq\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}

(B.50)

where the last inequality is by
3.2
b.c.
d\geq c_{3}
and
\gamma_{4}\lesssim\gamma_{2}
. By a similar argument,

\displaystyle|{D_{4,t}}|

\displaystyle=|\E_{w\sim\rho}[{P_{4,d}}(w)]-\beta\tau^{4}|

(B.51)

\displaystyle\leq\beta\tau^{4}+\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}+\delta^{4}+O\Big(\frac{1}{d}\Big)

(Using
|P_{4,d}(w)|\leq 1
,
\mathbb{P}(|\iota|>|\iota_{2}|)\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}
,
\delta\leq\frac{\tau^{4}}{16}
and
Equation

A.4
)

\displaystyle\leq(\beta+1/8)\tau^{4}

(B.c.
\delta\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}\leq\frac{\beta\tau^{4}}{16}
)

\displaystyle\leq|{D_{2,t}}|

(B.52)

Thus, for any time
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
, for any
\iota\in[\iota_{1},\iota_{2}]
, by
Lemma

4.4
(and because
w_{t}(\iota)\leq\delta
for any
\iota\in[\iota_{1},\iota_{2}]
by
Proposition

B.33
), we have

\displaystyle v_{t}(w_{t}(\iota))

\displaystyle=-(1-w_{t}(\iota)^{2})\cdot\Big(2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}{D_{2,t}}w_{t}(\iota)\cdot(1\pm O(\delta))+Q_{t}(w_{t}(\iota))\Big)

(B.c.
\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}\lesssim\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}
by
3.2
,
|D_{4,t}|\leq|D_{2,t}|
, and
w_{t}(\iota)\leq\delta
)

\displaystyle=2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|{D_{2,t}}|w_{t}(\iota)\cdot(1\pm O(\delta))-O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|}{d}\Big)|w_{t}(\iota)|

(B.c.
|w_{t}(\iota)|\leq\delta
and by bound on coefficients of
Q_{t}
from
Lemma

4.4
)

\displaystyle=2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|{D_{2,t}}|w_{t}(\iota)\cdot(1\pm O(\delta))

(B.c.
\delta\geq\frac{1}{d}
)

In summary,

\displaystyle v_{t}(w_{t}(\iota))

\displaystyle=2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|w_{t}(\iota)\cdot(1\pm O(\delta))

(B.53)

Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{v(w_{t}(\iota_{2}))}{w_{t}(\iota_{2})}\leq\frac{1+O(\delta)}{1-O(\delta)}\frac{v(w_{t}(\iota_{1}))}{w_{t}(\iota_{1})}\leq(1+O(\delta))\cdot\frac{v(w_{t}(\iota_{1}))}{w_{t}(\iota_{1})}

(B.54)

and
w_{t}(\iota_{2})
grows by a factor

\displaystyle F_{2}=\exp\Big(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{v(w_{t}(\iota_{2}))}{w_{t}(\iota_{2})}dt\Big)\leq\exp\Big((1+O(\delta))\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{v(w_{t}(\iota_{1}))}{w_{t}(\iota_{1})}dt\Big)=F_{1}^{1+O(\delta)}

(B.55)

Rearranging gives

\displaystyle F_{1}\geq F_{2}^{1-O(\delta)}=\frac{F_{2}}{F_{2}^{O(\delta)}}

(B.56)

Finally, observe that
F_{2}^{O(\delta)}\leq O(1)
, since by the assumption that
\delta\leq\frac{1}{\log F_{2}}
,

\displaystyle F_{2}^{\delta}

\displaystyle\leq F_{2}^{\frac{1}{\log F_{2}}}

(B.57)

and for any
x>0
,
x^{\frac{1}{\ln x}}=e
, meaning that
F_{2}^{\delta}\leq O(1)
. This implies that
F_{1}\gtrsim F_{2}
. From the above calculations, we can also obtain

\displaystyle\exp\Big(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|dt\Big)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\exp\Big(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{1}{1+O(\delta)}\cdot\frac{v_{t}(w_{t}(\iota_{2}))}{w_{t}(\iota_{2})}dt\Big)

(By
Equation

B.53
)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\exp\Big((1-O(\delta))\cdot\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{v_{t}(w_{t}(\iota_{2}))}{w_{t}(\iota_{2})}dt\Big)

(B.58)

\displaystyle\gtrsim F_{2}^{1-O(\delta)}

(B.59)

\displaystyle\gtrsim F_{2}

(By
Equation

B.57
)

as desired. Similarly, we can obtain

\displaystyle\exp\Big(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|dt\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\exp\Big(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(1+O(\delta))\cdot\frac{v_{t}(w_{t}(\iota_{2}))}{w_{t}(\iota_{2})}dt\Big)

(B.60)

\displaystyle\lesssim F_{2}^{1+O(\delta)}

(B.61)

\displaystyle\lesssim F_{2}

(B.c.
\delta\leq\frac{1}{\log F_{2}}
)

Finally,

\displaystyle F_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\exp\Big(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(1+O(\delta))\cdot\frac{v_{t}(w_{t}(\iota_{2}))}{w_{t}(\iota_{2})}dt\Big)

(B.62)

\displaystyle\lesssim F_{2}^{1+O(\delta)}

(B.63)

\displaystyle\lesssim F_{2}

(B.c.
\delta\leq\frac{1}{\log F_{2}}
)

as desired. Finally, we show the running time bound. Since
\delta\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}
and
\gamma_{2}
is less than a sufficiently small universal constant by
3.2
, for any time
t
during this interval,
v(w_{t}(\iota_{2}))\gtrsim\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2}(t)w_{t}(\iota_{2})\gtrsim\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}\tau^{2}w_{t}(\iota_{2})
(where the second inequality is by
Equation

B.50
). Thus, by Gronwall’s inequality (
G.13
), the time elapsed when
w_{t}(\iota_{2})
grows by a factor of
F_{2}
is at most
\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}\tau^{2}}\log F_{2}
.
∎

B.3.1

Proof of
Lemma

4.8

As a direct consequence of the above lemma, we can show
Lemma

4.8
, which characterizes how fast the particles
w_{t}
grow during Phase 1.

Proof of
Lemma

4.8
.

To show the lower bound on
w_{T_{1}}(\iota)
, we apply
Lemma

B.4
, with
t_{0}=0
and
t_{1}=T_{1}
, with
\delta=\frac{1}{\log d}
, and with
\iota_{1}=\iota
and
\iota_{2}=\iota_{\textup{U}}
. Let us verify that the assumptions of
Lemma

B.4
hold:

•

Observe that
\frac{w_{T_{1}}(\iota_{\textup{U}})}{\iota_{\textup{U}}}=\frac{1/\log d}{\log d/\sqrt{d}}=\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}
, meaning that
\log F_{2}\leq\log d
and
\frac{1}{\log F_{2}}\geq\delta
.

•

By
3.2
,
\delta=\frac{1}{\log d}\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}
, since
d\geq c_{3}
.

•

The assumption that
\mathbb{P}(|\iota|\geq\iota_{\textup{U}})\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}
holds by a standard bound on the tail of
\mu_{d}
.

Using the notation of
Lemma

B.4
,

\displaystyle F_{2}=\frac{w_{T_{1}}(\iota_{\textup{U}})}{\iota_{\textup{U}}}=\frac{{w_{\textup{max}}}}{(\log d)/\sqrt{d}}=\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}

(B.64)

where
{w_{\textup{max}}}
is as defined in
Definition

4.5
. By
Equation

B.46
, we therefore know that

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}\gtrsim\frac{w_{T_{1}}(\iota)}{\iota}\gtrsim\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}

(B.65)

For the bound on the running time, observe that by
Equation

B.48
,
T_{1}\lesssim\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}\gamma_{2}}\log\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}
. The final statement holds because
F_{2}=\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}
and from the second statement of
Lemma

B.4
. This completes the proof.
∎

B.4

Analysis of Phase 2

Recall the formal definition of Phase 2 from
Definition

4.6
. Our analysis in this section has two main goals: (1) to show that the running time of Phase 2 is small, and (2) to show that a majority of the particles become at least
\frac{1}{\log\log d}
in magnitude, up to a constant factor. We will achieve goal (2) using
Lemma

B.4
— then, we show that goal (1) holds using goal (2). Additionally, goal (2) will be useful for the subsequent phases as well.

First, the following proposition defines a range of particles which will be relevant during Phase 2 and Phase 3. This range of particles is a strict subset of the interval
(0,\iota_{\textup{U}})
which we considered during Phase 1 — we will show that this new range of particles grows uniformly during the beginning of Phase 2, until the largest of them becomes about
\frac{1}{\log\log d}
in magnitude. Specifically, we define
\iota_{\textup{L}}\triangleq\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{d}}
and
\iota_{\textup{R}}\triangleq\frac{1}{\kappa\sqrt{d}}
where
\kappa\asymp\frac{1}{\log\log d}
. The following proposition states that at initialization, a very large fraction of the
\iota
’s have magnitude between
\iota_{\textup{L}}
and
\iota_{\textup{R}}
.

Proposition B.5
.

Let
\kappa\in(0,1)
, and suppose
\iota
is drawn uniformly at random from
\mu_{d}
. Define
\iota_{\textup{L}}=\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{d}}
and
\iota_{\textup{R}}=\frac{1}{\kappa\sqrt{d}}.
Then,
\mathbb{P}(|\iota|\not\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}])\lesssim\kappa
.

Proof of
Proposition

B.5
.

First, we upper bound the probability that
|\iota|\geq\iota_{\textup{R}}
. By Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|\iota|\geq\iota_{\textup{R}})\leq\frac{\E[\iota^{2}]}{\iota_{\textup{R}}^{2}}=\frac{1/d}{1/(\kappa^{2}d)}=\kappa^{2}

(B.66)

Now, we upper bound the probability that
|\iota|\leq\iota_{\textup{L}}
. By Eqs. (1.16) and (1.18) of
Atkinson and Han 2012
, the probability density function of
\iota
is
p(\iota)=\frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma((d-1)/2)}(1-\iota^{2})^{\frac{d-3}{2}}
. Thus, we can simply upper bound the density by its maximum value:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|\iota|\leq\iota_{\textup{L}})

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma((d-1)/2)}\int_{-\iota_{\textup{L}}}^{\iota_{\textup{L}}}(1-t^{2})^{\frac{d-3}{2}}dt

(B.67)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma((d-1)/2)}\cdot 2\iota_{\textup{L}}

(B.68)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{d}\cdot\iota_{\textup{L}}

(By Stirling’s Formula)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{d}\cdot\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{d}}

(B.69)

\displaystyle\lesssim\kappa

(B.70)

as desired.
∎

Next, as a corollary of
Lemma

B.4
, we show that for a portion of Phase 2, the particles initialized at
\iota\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
grow by a similar factor. Specifically, this is true until the largest of these particles is
\xi=\frac{1}{2\log\log d}
in magnitude.

Lemma B.6

(Corollary of
Lemma

B.4
)
.

Suppose we are in the setting of
Theorem

3.3
. Let
{T_{\textup{R}}}>T_{1}
be the first time such that
w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota_{\textup{R}})=\xi=\frac{1}{2\log\log d}
. Then, for all
\iota
with
|\iota|\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
, we have
|w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota)|\gtrsim\xi\kappa^{2}
, and additionally,
\frac{w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota)}{w_{T_{1}}(\iota)}\asymp\xi\kappa(\log d)^{2}
. Furthermore,
{T_{\textup{R}}}-T_{1}\lesssim\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}\gamma_{2}}\log\log d
.

Proof of
Lemma

B.6
.

By
Lemma

4.8
,
w_{T_{1}}(\iota_{\textup{R}})\asymp\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}\iota_{\textup{R}}=\frac{1}{\kappa(\log d)^{2}}
. Let
\iota_{\textup{L}}=\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{d}}
and
\iota_{\textup{R}}=\frac{1}{\kappa\sqrt{d}}
, where
\kappa=\frac{1}{\log\log d}
. Now, we apply
Lemma

B.4
, with
t_{0}=T_{1}
and
t_{1}={T_{\textup{R}}}
, and with
\iota_{1}=\iota
for some
\iota\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
and
\iota_{2}=\iota_{\textup{R}}
, and with
\delta=\frac{1}{2\log\log d}
. Note that we assume without loss of generality that
\iota>0
— the statement for
\iota<0
follows by the symmetry of
\rho_{t}
. Let us verify that the assumptions hold:

•

First,
\delta=\frac{1}{2\log\log d}\gtrsim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}
. Addditionally, observe that by
Lemma

4.8
,
\frac{w_{T_{1}}(\iota_{\textup{R}})}{\iota_{\textup{R}}}\asymp\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}
, meaning that
w_{T_{1}}(\iota_{\textup{R}})\asymp\frac{1}{\kappa(\log d)^{2}}
. Thus, using the notation of
Lemma

B.4
, we have
F_{2}\lesssim\frac{1/\log\log d}{1/(\kappa(\log d)^{2})}=\frac{\kappa(\log d)^{2}}{\log\log d}
, and
\log F_{2}\leq 2\log\log d-2\log\log\log d+C<\frac{1}{\delta}
, as desired. (Here
C
is a universal constant, and the last inequality holds by
3.2
since
d\geq c_{3}
.)

•

By
3.2
,
\delta=\frac{1}{2\log\log d}\lesssim\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}
since
d\geq c_{3}
.

•

Additionally, the probability that
|\iota|\geq|\iota_{\textup{R}}|
is at most
\kappa=\frac{1}{\log\log d}
up to constant factors by
Proposition

B.5
, and this is clearly at most
\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}
by
3.2
.

Using
Lemma

B.4
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota)}{w_{T_{1}}(\iota)}\asymp\frac{w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota_{\textup{R}})}{w_{T_{1}}(\iota_{\textup{R}})}\asymp\frac{\xi}{w_{T_{1}}(\iota_{\textup{R}})}\asymp\frac{\xi}{\Big(\frac{1}{\kappa(\log d)^{2}}\Big)}\asymp\xi\kappa(\log d)^{2}

(B.71)

Thus, using the conclusion of
Lemma

4.8
that
\frac{w_{T_{1}}(\iota)}{\iota}\gtrsim\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota)\gtrsim\frac{w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota)}{w_{T_{1}}(\iota)}\cdot\frac{w_{T_{1}}(\iota)}{\iota}\cdot\iota\gtrsim\xi\kappa(\log d)^{2}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{d}}\gtrsim\xi\kappa^{2}

(B.72)

as desired. Thus, we obtain the first statement of this lemma. In addition, by
Equation

B.48
, and since
w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota_{\textup{R}})=\xi
and
w_{T_{1}}(\iota_{\textup{R}})\gtrsim\frac{1}{\kappa(\log d)^{2}}
, the running time bound we obtain for this part of Phase 2 is
{T_{\textup{R}}}-T_{1}\lesssim\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}\tau^{2}}\log\frac{\xi}{1/(\kappa(\log d)^{2})}\lesssim\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}\tau^{2}}(\log\log d)
, as desired.
∎

We now prove some auxiliary lemmas. First, we show an upper bound on
L(\rho_{0})
, which is needed in order to later show an upper bound on the time required to have
L(\rho_{t})\leq(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})\epsilon^{2}
. In the process, we also show a reasonably tight bound on the magnitude of
D_{2,t}
and
D_{4,t}
during the first part of Phase 2.

Lemma B.7
.

Suppose we are in the setting of
Theorem

3.3
and
Lemma

B.6
, and suppose
0\leq t\leq{T_{\textup{R}}}
. Then,
-\frac{3\gamma_{2}}{2}\leq D_{2,t}\leq-\frac{\gamma_{2}}{2}
and
-\frac{3\gamma_{4}}{2}\leq D_{4,t}\leq-\frac{\gamma_{4}}{2}
. As a corollary, for all
t\geq 0
,
L(\rho_{t})\lesssim(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})\gamma_{2}^{2}
.

Proof.

Since
t\leq{T_{\textup{R}}}
,
w_{t}(\iota_{\textup{R}})\leq\xi=\frac{1}{2\log\log d}
(because for
w\gtrsim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}
, we have
\frac{dw}{dt}\geq 0
by
Lemma

4.4
). By
Proposition

B.33
, for all
\iota
with
|\iota|\leq\iota_{\textup{R}}
,
|w_{t}(\iota)|\leq\xi
. Thus, by
Proposition

B.5
,

\displaystyle\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\iota\sim\rho_{0}}(|\iota|\leq\iota_{\textup{R}})\cdot\E[w^{2}\mid|\iota|\leq\iota_{\textup{R}}]+\mathbb{P}_{\iota\sim\rho_{0}}(|\iota|\geq\iota_{\textup{R}})\cdot\E[w^{2}\mid|\iota|\geq\iota_{\textup{R}}]

(B.73)

\displaystyle\leq\xi^{2}+O(\kappa)

(By
Proposition

B.5
)

Thus,
D_{2,t}=O(\xi^{2}+\kappa)-\gamma_{2}
, meaning that
-\frac{3\gamma_{2}}{2}\leq D_{2}\leq-\frac{\gamma_{2}}{2}
, and similarly,
-\frac{3\gamma_{4}}{2}\leq D_{4}\leq-\frac{\gamma_{4}}{2}
. The corollary follows from the fact that
\gamma_{4}\lesssim\gamma_{2}^{2}
by
3.2
.
∎

Next, we show that the particles are in fact increasing in magnitude during Phases 1 and 2. This makes use of our bound on the magnitudes of
D_{2,t}
and
D_{4,t}
up to time
{T_{\textup{R}}}
.

Lemma B.8
.

Suppose we are in the setting of
Theorem

3.3
, and suppose
[t_{0},t_{1}]
is a time interval with
0\leq t_{0}<t_{1}\leq T_{2}
. Then, for all
\iota
with
|\iota|\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
,
|w_{t}(\iota)|
is increasing on
[t_{0},t_{1}]
.

Proof.

Recall that by
Lemma

4.4
,

\displaystyle v_{t}(w)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})(P_{t}(w)+Q_{t}(w))

(B.74)

and for
w\geq 0
, it suffices to show that
P_{t}(w)+Q_{t}(w)\leq 0
. For
t\leq{T_{\textup{R}}}
,

\displaystyle P_{t}(w)+Q_{t}(w)

\displaystyle\leq 2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}w+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w^{3}+O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|}{d}\Big)|w|

(B.75)

\displaystyle\leq-\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}\gamma_{2}w+O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|}{d}\Big)|w|

(By
Lemma

B.7
, b.c.
D_{4,t}\leq 0
)

\displaystyle\leq 0

(B.c.
\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}\lesssim\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}
and
\gamma_{2}\gtrsim\frac{|D_{2,t}|}{d}
and
\gamma_{4}\gtrsim\frac{|D_{4,t}|}{d}
(
3.2
))

For
t\geq{T_{\textup{R}}}
, suppose
w\gtrsim\xi\kappa^{2}
(which holds at time
{T_{\textup{R}}}
for
\iota\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
by
Lemma

B.6
). Then,

\displaystyle P_{t}(w)+Q_{t}(w)

\displaystyle\leq 2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}w+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w^{3}+O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|}{d}\Big)|w|

(By
Lemma

4.4
)

\displaystyle\leq\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}\Big(2w-\frac{Cw}{d}\Big)+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}\Big(4w^{3}-\frac{Cw}{d}\Big)

(B.76)

for some universal constant
C>0
. The final expression on the right-hand side is negative — to see this, note that
2w-\frac{w}{d}>0
if
w\gtrsim\xi\kappa^{2}
, and
4w^{3}-\frac{w}{d}>0
if
w\gtrsim\xi\kappa^{2}
since
d\geq c_{3}
by
3.2
. This completes the proof of the lemma when
\iota\geq 0
. For
\iota<0
, the lemma holds by the symmetry of
\rho_{t}
.
∎

B.4.1

Proof of
Lemma

4.9

Finally, we show
Lemma

4.9
which concludes the analysis of Phase 2. For times
t\geq{T_{\textup{R}}}
within Phase 2, the particles with
|\iota|\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
satisfy
|w_{t}(\iota)|\gtrsim\xi\kappa^{2}
. Using this observation, and the fact that
D_{2,t}
and
D_{4,t}
have the same sign (meaning that the two terms of
P_{t}(w)=2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}w+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w^{3}
do not cancel) we show that the velocity
v(w)
is large on average. By
Lemma

B.35
, this allows us to show that the loss decreases quickly at any time
t\geq{T_{\textup{R}}}
in Phase 2, meaning that either the loss goes below
(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})\epsilon^{2}
quickly, or we exit Phase 2 quickly.

Proof of
Lemma

4.9
.

First, let
{T_{\textup{R}}}
be as defined in
Lemma

B.6
. By
Lemma

B.6
,
{T_{\textup{R}}}-T_{1}\lesssim\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}\gamma_{2}}\log\log d
. Furthermore, at time
{T_{\textup{R}}}
, for all
\iota
such that
|\iota|\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
,
|w_{t}(\iota)|\gtrsim\xi\kappa^{2}
.

Our proof strategy is now to show that
v(w)
is large on average, and then apply
Lemma

B.35
. At any time
t\in[{T_{\textup{R}}},T_{2}]
, since
D_{2,t}\leq 0
,
Proposition

B.34
implies that
\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{2}]\leq\gamma_{2}+O\Big(\frac{1}{d}\Big)
. Thus, by Markov’s inequality, and because
d\geq c_{3}
(where
c_{3}
is defined in
3.2
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(|w|\geq 1/2)\leq 4\cdot\Big(\gamma_{2}+\frac{1}{d}\Big)\leq 5\tau^{2}

(B.77)

For all
\iota
with
|\iota|\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
,
|w_{t}(\iota)|
is increasing as a function of
t
on
[0,T_{2}]
, by
Lemma

B.8
. Thus, by
Proposition

B.5
and a union bound,
\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(\xi\kappa^{2}\leq|w_{t}(\iota)|\leq 1/2)\geq 1-O(\kappa)-5\gamma_{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}
for all
t\geq{T_{\textup{R}}}
and all
\iota
with
|\iota|\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
. (Note that
1-O(\kappa)-5\gamma_{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}
holds because
\gamma_{2}
is less than a sufficiently small constant by
3.2
.) Finally, for all
w
satisfying
\xi\kappa^{2}\leq|w|\leq\frac{1}{2}
, using
Lemma

4.4
we can compute that

\displaystyle|v_{t}(w)|

\displaystyle\geq(1-w^{2})\cdot(|P_{t}(w)|-|Q_{t}(w)|)

(By
Lemma

4.4
)

\displaystyle\geq(1-w^{2})\cdot\Big|2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}||w|+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}||w^{3}|\Big|-O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|}{d}\Big)|w|

(By
Lemma

4.4
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{3}{4}\cdot(2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|\xi\kappa^{2}+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|\cdot\xi^{3}\kappa^{6})-O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|}{d}\Big)|w|

(B.c.
\xi\kappa^{2}\leq|w|\leq\frac{1}{2}
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot(2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|\xi\kappa^{2}+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|\cdot\xi^{3}\kappa^{6})

(B.c.
d
is sufficiently large (
3.2
))

Thus, by
Lemma

B.35
,

\displaystyle\frac{dL(\rho_{t})}{dt}

\displaystyle\lesssim-(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|\xi\kappa^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|\xi^{3}\kappa^{6})^{2}

(B.78)

\displaystyle\lesssim-\xi^{6}\kappa^{12}(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|)^{2}

(B.79)

\displaystyle\lesssim-\xi^{6}\kappa^{12}(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|)\cdot(|D_{2,t}|+|D_{4,t}|)

(B.80)

\displaystyle\lesssim-\xi^{6}\kappa^{12}(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|^{2})

(B.81)

\displaystyle\lesssim-\xi^{6}\kappa^{12}(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})L

(By
Lemma

4.2
)

Thus, since
L(\rho_{{T_{\textup{R}}}})\lesssim(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})\gamma_{2}
(by
Lemma

B.7
), by Gronwall’s inequality (
G.13
), we know that for
t\in[{T_{\textup{R}}},T_{2}]
,

\displaystyle L(\rho_{t})

\displaystyle\leq L(\rho_{{T_{\textup{R}}}})\cdot\exp\Big(\int_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}^{t}-\xi^{6}\kappa^{12}(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})ds\Big)

(B.82)

\displaystyle=L(\rho_{{T_{\textup{R}}}})e^{-(t-{T_{\textup{R}}})\xi^{6}\kappa^{12}(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})}

(B.83)

Thus, if
L(\rho_{t})\gtrsim(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})\epsilon^{2}
, this implies that

\displaystyle e^{-(t-{T_{\textup{R}}})\xi^{6}\kappa^{12}(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}

(B.84)

and rearranging gives
t-{T_{\textup{R}}}\leq\frac{1}{(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})\xi^{6}\kappa^{12}}\log(\frac{\gamma_{2}}{\epsilon^{2}})
. Thus, either
T_{2}-{T_{\textup{R}}}\leq\frac{1}{(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})\xi^{6}\kappa^{12}}\log(\frac{\gamma_{2}}{\epsilon^{2}})
, or there exists a time
t\in[{T_{\textup{R}}},T_{2}]
such that
L(\rho_{t})\lesssim(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})\epsilon^{2}
, and such that
t-{T_{\textup{R}}}\leq\frac{1}{(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})\xi^{6}\kappa^{12}}\log(\frac{\gamma_{2}}{\epsilon})
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

B.5

Phase 3, Case 1

Phase 3, Case 1 is the case where
D_{2,T_{2}}=0
and
D_{4,T_{2}}<0
, i.e. the case where
D_{2,t}
reaches
0
first. As we later show in
Lemma

B.17
and
Lemma

B.18
, if this occurs, then for all
t\geq T_{2}
, it will be the case that
D_{2,t}\geq 0
and
D_{4,t}\leq 0
. We now outline our analysis of this case — the goal of our analysis is to show that either
|D_{2,t}|\leq\epsilon
or
|D_{4,t}|\leq\epsilon
holds within
\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}}\cdot\textup{poly}\Big(\frac{\log\log d}{\epsilon}\Big)
time after the start of Phase 3, Case 1.

Proof Strategy for Phase 3, Case 1

Our overall proof strategy in this section is to show that
|v(w)|
is large for a large portion of particles
w
, and then to apply
Lemma

B.35
to show that the loss decreases quickly. Recall that

\displaystyle v_{t}(w)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})(P_{t}(w)+Q_{t}(w))

(B.85)

and intuitively, we can ignore
Q_{t}(w)
due to the factor of
\frac{1}{d}
in its coefficients. Thus, we can write

\displaystyle v_{t}(w)

\displaystyle\approx-(1-w^{2})P_{t}(w)

(B.86)

\displaystyle\approx-(1-w^{2})\cdot w\cdot(2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w^{2})\,.

(B.87)

Since
D_{4,t}<0
and
D_{2,t}>0
, we can further rewrite the above as

\displaystyle v_{t}(w)

\displaystyle\approx-(1-w^{2})\cdot w\cdot 4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|(w-r)(w+r)

(B.88)

where
r=\sqrt{-\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}}{2\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}}}
is the positive root of the last factor
(2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w^{2})
. Thus, to show that the velocity
v_{T}(w)
is large, we want to show that there is a large fraction of particles
w\in[0,1]
simultaneously satisfying the following: (1)
w
is far from
1
, (2)
w
is far from
0
, and (3)
w
is far from
r
. Indeed, in
Lemma

B.15
, we show that out of the particles
w\in[0,1]
, at least a
\frac{\gamma_{2}}{2}
fraction of these particles is in
[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]
, enabling us to bound the distance of these particles
w
from
0
and
1
.

Thus, to show that
v_{t}(w)
is large for these particles, it suffices to show that
|w-r|
is large on average for the particles
w\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]
, i.e. the conditional expectation
\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[(w-r)^{2}\mid w\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]]
is large. In turn, this is at least the conditional variance of
w
conditioned on
w
being in
[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]
, so it suffices to show that
\Var(w\mid w\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}])
is large, and by
Proposition

B.36
it suffices to show that
\E[(w-w^{\prime})^{2}\mid w,w^{\prime}\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]]
is large.

To show that the quantity
\E[(w-w^{\prime})^{2}\mid w,w^{\prime}\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]]
is large, intuitively we would like to show that during Phase 3, Case 1,
w
and
w^{\prime}
are getting farther apart. However, this is not true. If we write the velocity as
v_{t}(w)=(1-w^{2})\cdot w\cdot(4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|w^{2}-2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|)
, then the last factor is indeed increasing as a function of
w\in[0,1]
, and thus “pushes” the different particles
w,w^{\prime}
further apart. This does not work as a formal argument, because as two particles
w,w^{\prime}
become closer to
0
or
1
, their distance
|w-w^{\prime}|
will decrease again due to the factors
w
and
(1-w^{2})
in the velocity. However, we are only concerned with particles
w\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]
— we must make precise the intuition that the behavior of the particles very close to
0
or
1
does not matter.

To make this proof strategy precise, we define the potential function
\Phi(w)=\log\Big(\frac{w}{\sqrt{1-w^{2}}}\Big)
in
Definition

B.9
and show that
|\Phi(w)-\Phi(w^{\prime})|
is increasing in Phase 3, Case 1. As shown in
Lemma

B.10
, we have
\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(w)\approx-2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}-4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w^{2}
— this is simply because
\frac{d\Phi(w)}{dw}=\frac{1}{w(1-w^{2})}
. In other words,
\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(w)
is exactly
v(w)
, but without the factors
w
and
(1-w^{2})
— we have selected this potential function
\Phi(w)
specifically to eliminate these factors. Using this observation, it is easy to show that
|\Phi(w)-\Phi(w^{\prime})|
is increasing and thus obtain a lower bound on
|\Phi(w)-\Phi(w^{\prime})|
for a large fraction of pairs
w,w^{\prime}
. Finally, to convert this to a lower bound on
|w-w^{\prime}|
, we use the fact that
\Phi
is Lipschitz on the interval
[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]
(
Lemma

B.12
), which gives us
|w-w^{\prime}|\geq\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}^{O(1)}}|\Phi(w)-\Phi(w^{\prime})|
.

We now repeat the definition of the potential function:

Definition B.9

(Potential Function
\Phi
)
.

We define
\Phi:[0,1]\to(-\infty,\infty)
by
\Phi(w)=\log\Big(\frac{w}{\sqrt{1-w^{2}}}\Big)
.

We note that
\frac{d\Phi(w)}{dw}=\frac{1}{w(1-w^{2})}
. Next, we note the following useful fact about the potential function (we note that this does not require any assumption that we are in Phase 3, Case 1, and just follows from algebraic manipulations):

Lemma B.10
.

In the setting of
Theorem

3.3
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(w_{t})

\displaystyle=-2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}-4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w^{2}-{\lambda_{d}}^{(1)}-{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}w^{2}

(B.89)

where
{\lambda_{d}}^{(1)}
and
{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}
are as in the statement of
Lemma

4.4
.

Proof of
Lemma

B.10
.

For any
w_{t}
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(w_{t})=\frac{d\Phi}{dw}\cdot\frac{dw_{t}}{dt}=\frac{1}{w(1-w^{2})}\cdot\frac{dw_{t}}{dt}

(B.90)

Thus, writing
\frac{dw_{t}}{dt}=-(1-w^{2})\cdot(P_{t}(w)+Q_{t}(w))
where
P_{t}
and
Q_{t}
are defined in
Lemma

4.4
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(w_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w(1-w^{2})}\cdot\frac{dw_{t}}{dt}

(B.91)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{w}\cdot(P_{t}(w)+Q_{t}(w))

(B.92)

\displaystyle=-\frac{P_{t}(w)}{w}-\frac{Q_{t}(w)}{w}

(B.93)

\displaystyle=-2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}-4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w^{2}-\frac{Q_{t}(w)}{w}

(B.94)

\displaystyle=-2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}-4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w^{2}-{\lambda_{d}}^{(1)}-{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}w^{2}

(B.95)

where
{\lambda_{d}}^{(1)}
and
{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}
are as in the statement of
Lemma

4.4
.
∎

Intuitively, when
D_{2}>0
and
D_{4}<0
, the main part of the velocity is
P_{t}(w)=-2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}w-4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w^{3}
which is an upward sloping cubic polynomial — thus, the particles
w
move away from the positive root of this polynomial, and the distance between any pair
w,w^{\prime}
of particles is increasing provided that
w
and
w^{\prime}
are not too close to
0
or
1
. In the following few lemmas, we make this intuition formal using the potential function
\Phi
. We first show that for two particles
w,w^{\prime}
, the distance between
\Phi(w)
and
\Phi(w^{\prime})
is increasing as long as
D_{4,t}\leq 0
. Note that this condition holds not only during Phase 3, Case 1, but also during Phases 1 and 2.

Lemma B.11

(
\Phi(w)
and
\Phi(w^{\prime})
Moving Apart)
.

In the setting of
Theorem

3.3
, suppose
D_{4,t}\leq 0
for all
t
in some time interval
[t_{0},t_{1}]
, and let
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}>0
. Then,

\displaystyle\Big|\Phi(w_{t}(\iota_{1}))-\Phi(w_{t}(\iota_{2}))\Big|

(B.96)

is increasing on the interval
[t_{0},t_{1}]
.

Proof of
Lemma

B.11
.

Suppose
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in[0,1]
such that
\iota_{1}<\iota_{2}
— by
Proposition

B.33
, we will always have
w_{t}(\iota_{1})\leq w_{t}(\iota_{2})
. For convenience, in the rest of this proof, we will write
w_{1,t}=w_{t}(\iota_{1})
and
w_{2,t}=w_{t}(\iota_{2})
. Since
\Phi
is an increasing function of
w
, it will also always be the case that
\Phi(w_{2,t})\geq\Phi(w_{1,t})
. Thus, to show that
|\Phi(w_{2,t})-\Phi(w_{1,t})|
is increasing, it suffices to show that
\Phi(w_{2,t})-\Phi(w_{1,t})
is increasing. By
Lemma

B.10
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Big(\Phi(w_{2,t})-\Phi(w_{1,t})\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(-2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}-4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w_{2,t}^{2}-{\lambda_{d}}^{(1)}-{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}w_{2,t}^{2}\Big)

(B.97)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,-\Big(-2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}-4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w_{1,t}^{2}-{\lambda_{d}}^{(1)}-{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}w_{1,t}^{2}\Big)

(B.98)

\displaystyle=-4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}(w_{2,t}^{2}-w_{1,t}^{2})-{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}(w_{2,t}^{2}-w_{1,t}^{2})

(B.99)

\displaystyle=4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|(w_{2,t}^{2}-w_{1,t}^{2})-{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}(w_{2,t}^{2}-w_{1,t}^{2})

(B.100)

where
{\lambda_{d}}^{(1)}
and
{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}
are as in
Lemma

4.4
. Recall from the statement of
Lemma

4.4
that
|{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}|\lesssim\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|\cdot\frac{1}{d}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Big(\Phi(w_{2,t})-\Phi(w_{1,t})\Big)

\displaystyle=4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|(w_{2,t}^{2}-w_{1,t}^{2})-{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}(w_{2,t}^{2}-w_{1,t}^{2})

(B.101)

\displaystyle\geq 3\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|(w_{2,t}^{2}-w_{1,t}^{2})

(B.102)

and the right hand side is nonnegative since
w_{2,t}\geq w_{1,t}\geq 0
. Thus,
\Phi(w_{2,t})-\Phi(w_{1,t})
is increasing, as desired.
∎

Next, we prove a helpful lemma showing that the potential function is bi-Lipschitz. This is useful both when we show that
|\Phi(w)-\Phi(w^{\prime})|
is initially large (as it allows us to leverage an initial lower bound on
|w-w^{\prime}|
) and when we show that
|w-w^{\prime}|
is large later during Phase 3, Case 1 (as it allows us to leverage the lower bound we obtain on
|\Phi(w)-\Phi(w^{\prime})|
).

Lemma B.12
.

For all
w,w^{\prime}\in[0,1]
,
|\Phi(w)-\Phi(w^{\prime})|\geq|w-w^{\prime}|
. Additionally, for
\eta<\frac{1}{2}
and
w,w^{\prime}\in[\eta,1-\eta]
,
|\Phi(w)-\Phi(w^{\prime})|\leq\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}|w-w^{\prime}|
.

Proof of
Lemma

B.12
.

First, for all
w\in[0,1]
,
\frac{d\Phi(w)}{dw}=\frac{1}{w(1-w^{2})}>1
, and the first statement of the lemma follows. For the second statement of the lemma, observe that for
w\in[\eta,1-\eta]
,

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{d\Phi(w)}{dw}\Big|\leq\frac{1}{w(1-w^{2})}\leq\frac{1}{\eta(1-(1-\eta)^{2})}=\frac{1}{\eta(1-(1-2\eta+\eta^{2}))}=\frac{1}{\eta(2\eta-\eta^{2})}<\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}

(B.103)

where the last equality is because
\eta>\eta^{2}
(so
2\eta-\eta^{2}>\eta
). Thus,
\Phi
is
\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}
-Lipschitz on the interval
[\eta,1-\eta]
.
∎

We next need to show that the potential initially has a large value, at the beginning of Phase 3, Case 1. We show this in the next two lemmas, by showing that for any two particles
w,w^{\prime}
, the distance between them grows by a large factor during Phase 2 (specifically, by time
{T_{\textup{R}}}
) and then using this to obtain a lower bound on
|\Phi(w)-\Phi(w^{\prime})|
by the bi-Lipschitzness of
\Phi
.

Lemma B.13
.

In the setting of
Theorem

3.3
, let
{T_{\textup{R}}}
be as defined in
Lemma

B.6
. Then, for
\iota,\iota^{\prime}\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
,

\displaystyle\frac{w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota^{\prime})-w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota)}{\iota^{\prime}-\iota}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\xi\kappa\sqrt{d}

(B.104)

Proof of
Lemma

B.13
.

Let
\iota,\iota^{\prime}\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
, with
\iota<\iota^{\prime}
. For convenience, we will write
w_{t}=w_{t}(\iota)
and
w_{t}^{\prime}=w_{t}(\iota^{\prime})
. Our goal is to obtain a lower bound on the factor by which
w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t}
grows by time
{T_{\textup{R}}}
. First, define
P_{t}
and
Q_{t}
as in
Lemma

4.4
, and observe that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}(w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t})

\displaystyle=-(1-(w_{t}^{\prime})^{2})(P_{t}(w_{t}^{\prime})+Q_{t}(w_{t}^{\prime}))-(1-w_{t}^{2})(P_{t}(w_{t})+Q_{t}(w_{t}))

(B.105)

\displaystyle=-\Big(P_{t}(w_{t}^{\prime})-P_{t}(w_{t})\Big)+\Big((w_{t}^{\prime})^{2}P_{t}(w_{t}^{\prime})-w_{t}^{2}P_{t}(w_{t})\Big)-\Big(Q_{t}(w_{t}^{\prime})-Q_{t}(w_{t})\Big)

(B.106)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\Big((w_{t}^{\prime})^{2}Q_{t}(w_{t}^{\prime})-w_{t}^{2}Q_{t}(w_{t})\Big)

(B.107)

We first obtain a lower bound on the first term
-\Big(P_{t}(w_{t}^{\prime})-P_{t}(w_{t})\Big)
, and then show that the other terms are lower-order terms as long as
t\leq{T_{\textup{R}}}
. For
t\in[0,{T_{\textup{R}}}]
,

\displaystyle-(P_{t}(w_{t}^{\prime})-P_{t}(w_{t}))

\displaystyle=-\Big(2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}w_{t}^{\prime}+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}(w_{t}^{\prime})^{3}\Big)+\Big(2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}w_{t}+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w_{t}^{3}\Big)

(
Lemma

4.4
)

\displaystyle=-2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}(w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t})-4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}((w_{t}^{\prime})^{3}-w_{t}^{3})

(B.108)

\displaystyle=2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|(w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t})+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|((w_{t}^{\prime})^{3}-w_{t}^{3})

(B.c.
t\leq{T_{\textup{R}}}
,
D_{2,t},D_{4,t}<0
by
Lemma

B.7
)

\displaystyle=2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|(w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t})+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|((w_{t}^{\prime})^{2}+w_{t}w_{t}^{\prime}+w_{t}^{2})(w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t})

(B.109)

\displaystyle\geq 2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|(w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t})

(B.c. omitted term is nonnegative —
w_{t}^{\prime}>w_{t}
by
Proposition

B.33
)

Next, let us show that the remaining terms in
Equation

B.107
are lower-order terms which will not decrease the growth rate of
w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t}
too much. First let us deal with the absolute value of the second term in
Equation

B.107
. For convenience, let
w_{R,t}=w_{t}(\iota_{\textup{R}})
. Then,

\displaystyle\Big|(w_{t}^{\prime})^{2}P_{t}(w_{t}^{\prime})-w_{t}^{2}P_{t}(w_{t})\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big|(2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}(w_{t}^{\prime})^{3}+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}(w_{t}^{\prime})^{5})

(B.110)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,-(2\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{2,t}(w_{t})^{3}+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}(w_{t})^{5})\Big|

(B.111)

\displaystyle\lesssim 2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}||(w_{t}^{\prime})^{3}-w_{t}^{3}|+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}||(w_{t}^{\prime})^{5}-w_{t}^{5}|

(By
Lemma

4.4
)

\displaystyle\lesssim 2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}||(w_{t}^{\prime})^{2}+w_{t}^{\prime}w_{t}+w_{t}^{2}||w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t}|

(B.112)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}||(w_{t}^{\prime})^{4}+(w_{t}^{\prime})^{3}w_{t}+(w_{t}^{\prime})^{2}w_{t}^{2}+w_{t}^{\prime}w_{t}^{3}+w_{t}^{4}||w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t}|

(B.113)

\displaystyle\lesssim\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|w_{R,t}^{2}|w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t}|

(B.c.
|w_{t}|,|w_{t}^{\prime}|\leq w_{R,t}
by
Proposition

B.33
)

where the last inequality is also because
\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}\lesssim\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}
, and
\gamma_{4}\lesssim\gamma_{2}^{2}
(
3.2
) and
|D_{2}|\geq\frac{\gamma_{2}}{2}
and
|D_{4}|\leq\frac{3\gamma_{4}}{2}
for
t\leq{T_{\textup{R}}}
(
Lemma

B.7
), meaning that the first term in
Equation

B.113
dominates up to universal constant factors. Next, let us deal with the absolute value of the third term in
Equation

B.107
. By
Lemma

G.16
,

\displaystyle|Q_{t}(w_{t})^{\prime}-Q_{t}(w_{t})|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|}{d}\cdot|w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t}|

(By definition of
{\lambda_{d}}^{(1)},{\lambda_{d}}^{(3)}
,
Lemma

4.4
)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|}{d}|w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t}|

(B.c.
\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}\lesssim\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}
and
|D_{4}|\lesssim\gamma_{4}\lesssim\gamma_{2}^{2}\lesssim|D_{2}|
for
t\leq{T_{\textup{R}}}
(
Lemma

B.7
))

Using the same argument with
Lemma

G.16
, we can bound the fourth term in
Equation

B.107
:

\displaystyle|(w_{t}^{\prime})^{2}Q_{t}(w_{t}^{\prime})-w_{t}^{2}Q_{t}(w_{t})|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|}{d}|w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t}|

(B.114)

Combining the bounds we obtained for all the terms of
Equation

B.107
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}(w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t})

\displaystyle\geq 2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|(w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t})-O\Big(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|\Big(w_{R,t}^{2}+1/d\Big)(w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t})\Big)

(B.115)

\displaystyle=\Big(2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|-O\Big(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|(w_{R,t}^{2}+1/d)\Big)\Big)(w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t})

(B.116)

and therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{d}{dt}(w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t})}{w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t}}

\displaystyle\geq 2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|-O\Big(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|(w_{R,t}^{2}+1/d)\Big)

(B.117)

First, let us consider how much
w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t}
grows during Phase 1. At this point, we apply
Lemma

B.4
with
\iota_{2}=\iota_{\textup{U}}
and
0<\iota_{1}<\iota_{\textup{U}}
, with
\delta=\frac{1}{\log d}
. Let us first verify that the assumptions of
Lemma

B.4
are satisfied. By
Lemma

4.8
,
F_{2}\asymp\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}
, using the notation of
Lemma

B.4
. Thus,
\log F_{2}\leq\log d
, meaning
\delta\leq\frac{1}{\log F_{2}}
. By
3.2
,
\delta\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}
, and by
Proposition

G.5
,
\mathbb{P}(|\iota|>\iota_{\textup{U}})\leq\frac{1}{\log d}\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}
clearly holds. Thus, the assumptions of
Lemma

B.4
are satisfied. As a consequence of
Lemma

B.4
(specifically
Equation

B.47
) we obtain

\displaystyle\exp\Big(\int_{0}^{T_{1}}2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|dt\Big)\gtrsim F_{2}\gtrsim\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}

(B.118)

For
t\leq T_{1}
, we have
w_{R,t}^{2}\leq\delta^{2}\leq O(\frac{1}{\log d})
by
Definition

4.5
(and because
w_{t}(\iota_{\textup{R}})\leq w_{t}(\iota_{\textup{U}})
by
Proposition

B.33
). Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{w_{T_{1}}^{\prime}-w_{T_{1}}}{w_{0}^{\prime}-w_{0}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\exp\Big(\int_{0}^{T_{1}}2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|\cdot\Big(1-O\Big(\frac{1}{\log d}\Big)\Big)dt\Big)

(By
Equation

B.117
)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\Big(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}\Big)^{1-O(1/\log d)}

(By
Equation

B.118
)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}

(B.c.
d^{1/\log d}\leq O(1)
)

Additionally, we apply
Lemma

B.4
with
\iota_{2}=\iota_{\textup{R}}
(and an arbitrary
\iota_{1}\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
) with
\delta=\xi=\frac{1}{2\log\log d}
, and
t_{0}=T_{1}
and
t_{1}
being the time
{T_{\textup{R}}}
such that
w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota_{\textup{R}})=\xi
. We verify that the conditions of
Lemma

B.4
hold. Using the notation of
Lemma

B.4
,
F_{2}=\frac{w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota_{\textup{R}})}{w_{T_{1}}(\iota_{\textup{R}})}=\xi\kappa(\log d)^{2}
by
Lemma

B.6
, and thus
\log F_{2}\leq\frac{1}{\delta}
. Again by
3.2
,
\delta\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}
, and by
Proposition

B.5
,
\mathbb{P}(|\iota|>\iota_{\textup{R}})\leq O(\kappa)\leq\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{16}
. Thus, we apply
Lemma

B.4
to obtain

\displaystyle\exp\Big(\int_{T_{1}}^{T}2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|dt\Big)\gtrsim F_{2}\gtrsim\xi\kappa(\log d)^{2}

(B.119)

For
t\leq{T_{\textup{R}}}
,
w_{R,t}^{2}\leq\xi^{2}\leq\delta^{2}
, meaning that

\displaystyle\frac{w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}^{\prime}-w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}}{w_{T_{1}}^{\prime}-w_{T_{1}}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\exp\Big(\int_{T_{1}}^{{T_{\textup{R}}}}2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|\cdot(1-O(\delta^{2}))dt\Big)

(By
Equation

B.117
)

\displaystyle\gtrsim(\xi\kappa(\log d)^{2})^{1-O(\delta^{2})}

(By
Equation

B.119
)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\xi\kappa(\log d)^{2}

(B.c.
(\log d)^{O(\delta)}=(\log d)^{O(\frac{1}{\log\log d})}\leq O(1)
)

In summary, since
w_{t}^{\prime}-w_{t}
grows by
\frac{\sqrt{d}}{(\log d)^{2}}
(up to a constant factor) from time
0
to time
T_{1}
, and by
\xi\kappa(\log d)^{2}
(up to a constant factor) from time
T_{1}
to time
{T_{\textup{R}}}
, this means

\displaystyle\frac{w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}^{\prime}-w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}}{w_{0}^{\prime}-w_{0}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\xi\kappa\sqrt{d}

(B.120)

from time
0
to time
{T_{\textup{R}}}
, as desired.
∎

We now use the previous lemma to obtain a lower bound on
|\Phi(w)-\Phi(w^{\prime})|
by using the bi-Lipschitzness of
\Phi
.

Lemma B.14

(Initial Large Distance Between
w
and
w^{\prime}
)
.

Suppose we are in the setting of
Theorem

3.3
. Let
{T_{\textup{R}}}
be as in the statement of
Lemma

B.6
. If
\iota,\iota^{\prime}
are sampled independently from
\rho_{0}
, then with probability at least
1-O(\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle|\Phi(w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota))-\Phi(w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota^{\prime}))|\gtrsim\xi\kappa^{3}

(B.121)

Proof of
Lemma

B.14
.

By Eqs. (1.16) and (1.18) of
Atkinson and Han 2012
, the probability density function of
\iota
is
p(\iota)=\frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma((d-1)/2)}(1-\iota^{2})^{\frac{d-3}{2}}
. Thus, for
\iota\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
, the conditional density function
p(\iota\mid\iota\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}])
can be upper bounded as

\displaystyle p(\iota\mid\iota\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}])

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{p(\iota)}{p(\iota\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}])}

(B.122)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma((d-1)/2)}\cdot\frac{(1-\iota^{2})^{\frac{d-3}{2}}}{p(\iota\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}])}

(B.123)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma((d-1)/2)}\cdot\frac{(1-\iota^{2})^{\frac{d-3}{2}}}{1-O(\kappa)}

(By
Proposition

B.5
)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{d}

(By Stirling’s Formula)

In particular, for any interval
I
of length at most
\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{d}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\iota\in I\mid\iota\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}])\lesssim\sqrt{d}\cdot|I|\lesssim\kappa^{2}

(B.124)

and from this it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\iota,\iota^{\prime}\sim\rho_{0}}\Big(|\iota-\iota^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{d}}\mid\iota,\iota^{\prime}\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\kappa^{2}

(B.125)

Next, suppose
\iota,\iota^{\prime}\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
with
\iota^{\prime}>\iota
, and
|\iota-\iota^{\prime}|\geq\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{d}}
. Then, by
Lemma

B.13
, if
{T_{\textup{R}}}
is the time
T
mentioned in the statement of
Lemma

B.6
, then

\displaystyle w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota^{\prime})-w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\xi\kappa^{3}

(B.126)

Suppose we sample
\iota,\iota^{\prime}
independently from
\rho_{0}
. By
Proposition

B.5
, with probability at least
1-O(\kappa)
,
\iota,\iota^{\prime}\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
. Thus, by
Equation

B.125
, the probability that
\iota,\iota^{\prime}\in[\iota_{\textup{L}},\iota_{\textup{R}}]
and
|\iota-\iota^{\prime}|\geq\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\sqrt{d}}
is at least
1-O(\kappa)
. In summary, if we sample
\iota,\iota^{\prime}
independently from
\rho_{0}
, then with probability
1-O(\kappa)
,

\displaystyle|\Phi(w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota))-\Phi(w_{{T_{\textup{R}}}}(\iota^{\prime}))|\gtrsim\xi\kappa^{3}

(B.127)

by
Lemma

B.12
, as desired.
∎

Next, we show that during Phase 3, Case 1, at any time, there is a significant fraction of particles whose distance from
0
or
1
can be bounded below.

Lemma B.15
.

In the setting of
Theorem

3.3
, suppose
D_{4,t}\leq 0
and
D_{2,t}\geq 0
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(|w|\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}])\geq\frac{\gamma_{2}}{2}

(B.128)

Proof of
Lemma

B.15
.

For convenience, we define
\tau=\sqrt{\gamma_{2}}
and
\beta\geq 1.1
such that
\gamma_{4}=\beta\tau^{4}
. (Here,
\beta
and
\tau
are well-defined by
3.2
.) First, since
D_{2,t}\geq 0
during Phase 3 Case 1, we know that

\displaystyle\E_{w_{t}\sim\rho_{t}}[{P_{2,d}}(w)]

\displaystyle\geq\tau^{2}

(By Definition of
D_{2,t}
)

and since
D_{4,t}\leq 0
during Phase 3 Case 1, we know that

\displaystyle\E_{w_{t}\sim\rho_{t}}[{P_{4,d}}(w)]

\displaystyle\leq\beta\tau^{4}

(By Definition of
D_{4,t}
)

Rearranging using
{P_{2,d}}(t)=\frac{d}{d-1}t^{2}-\frac{1}{d-1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d-1}\E_{w_{t}\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{2}]-\frac{1}{d-1}\geq\tau^{2}

(B.129)

or

\displaystyle\E_{w_{t}\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{2}]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{d-1}{d}\tau^{2}+\frac{1}{d}

(B.130)

Similarly, rearranging using
{P_{4,d}}(t)=\frac{d^{2}+6d+8}{d^{2}-1}t^{4}-\frac{6d+12}{d^{2}-1}t^{2}+\frac{3}{d^{2}-1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}+6d+8}{d^{2}-1}\E_{w_{t}\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{4}]-\frac{6d+12}{d^{2}-1}\E_{w_{t}\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{2}]+\frac{3}{d^{2}-1}\leq\beta\tau^{4}

(B.131)

and rearranging gives

\displaystyle\E_{w_{t}\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{4}]

\displaystyle\leq\beta\tau^{4}+O\Big(\frac{1}{d}\Big)

(B.132)

because
w\leq 1
and we can assume
\beta\tau^{4}\leq 1
due to
3.2
. Suppose that with probability more than
1-\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}
under
\rho_{t}
,
w\not\in[\tau^{3/2},1-\tau]
. Then,

\displaystyle\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(w\geq 1-\tau)\cdot\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{2}\mid w\geq 1-\tau]

(B.133)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(w\in[\tau^{3/2},1-\tau])\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{2}\mid w\in[\tau^{3/2},1-\tau]]

(B.134)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(w\leq\tau^{3/2})\cdot\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{2}\mid w\leq\tau^{3/2}]

(B.135)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(w\geq 1-\tau)+\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}+\tau^{3}

(By assumption that
\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(w\in[\tau^{3/2},1-\tau])\leq\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}
)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(w\geq 1-\tau)+\frac{3\tau^{2}}{4}

(B.c.
\tau\leq 1/4
(by
3.2
,
\tau
is sufficiently small))

which by
Equation

B.130
implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(w\geq 1-\tau)\geq\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{2}]-\frac{3\tau^{2}}{4}\geq\frac{d-1}{d}\tau^{2}+\frac{1}{d}-\frac{3\tau^{2}}{4}=\frac{\tau^{2}}{4}-\frac{\tau^{2}}{d}+\frac{1}{d}\geq\frac{\tau^{2}}{4}

(B.136)

where the last inequality is because
\tau\leq 1
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{4}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(w\geq 1-\tau)\cdot\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{4}\mid w\geq 1-\tau]

(B.137)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(w\in[\tau^{3/2},1-\tau])\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{4}\mid w\in[\tau^{3/2},1-\tau]]

(B.138)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(w\leq\tau^{3/2})\cdot\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{4}\mid w\leq\tau^{3/2}]

(B.139)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(w\geq 1-\tau)\cdot\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{4}\mid w\geq 1-\tau]

(B.140)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\tau)^{4}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(w\geq 1-\tau)

(B.141)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\tau)^{4}\cdot\frac{\tau^{2}}{4}

(By
Equation

B.136
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\tau^{2}}{64}

(B.c.
\tau\leq 1/2
(see
3.2
))

By
Equation

B.132
, this is a contradiction, since it implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\tau^{2}}{64}\leq\beta\tau^{4}+O\Big(\frac{1}{d}\Big)\leq 2\beta\tau^{4}

(B.142)

where the second inequality is because
\beta\geq 1.1
and
d
is sufficiently large (see
3.2
). This implies that
128\beta\tau^{2}\geq 1
, which contradicts
3.2
(since
\gamma_{2}
is chosen to be sufficiently small). Thus, our original assumption that
\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(w\in[\tau^{3/2},1-\tau])\leq\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}
is incorrect.
∎

The purpose of the next three lemmas is to show that if Phase 3, Case 1 occurs, i.e. if
D_{2,T_{2}}=0
and
D_{4,T_{2}}<0
, then for all
t\geq T_{2}
, we have
D_{2,t}\geq 0
and
D_{4,t}\leq 0
. First, the following lemma states that if
D_{4,t}
is much smaller than
D_{2,t}
in absolute value, and
D_{4,t}<0
and
D_{2,t}>0
, then the velocity of the particles
w\geq 0
will be significantly negative.

Lemma B.16
.

Suppose we are in the setting of
Theorem

3.3
, and suppose
D_{4,T_{2}}=0
and
t\geq T_{2}
such that
D_{4,t}\leq 0
and
D_{2,t}\geq 0
. If
|D_{4,t}|\leq\xi|D_{2,t}|
where
\xi=\frac{1}{\log\log d}
, then for all
w\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle v_{t}(w)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})\cdot(1\pm O(\xi))\cdot 2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|w

(B.143)

In particular, for
w\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]
,

\displaystyle v_{t}(w)

\displaystyle\lesssim-\gamma_{2}^{5/4}\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|

(B.144)

Proof.

Suppose
|D_{4,t}|\leq\xi|D_{2,t}|
where
\xi=\frac{1}{\log\log d}
. Let
P_{t}
and
Q_{t}
be as in
Lemma

4.4
. Then, for any
w\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle P_{t}(w)

\displaystyle=(2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}w+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w^{3})

(B.145)

\displaystyle=(2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|w-4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|w^{3})

(B.146)

\displaystyle=\Big(2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|w\pm O(\xi\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|w^{3})\Big)

(B.147)

\displaystyle=(1\pm O(\xi))\cdot 2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|w

(By
3.2
,
\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}\lesssim\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}
)

Thus, for
w\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle v_{t}(w)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})(P_{t}(w)+Q_{t}(w))

(By
Lemma

4.4
)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})\Big(|P_{t}(w)|\pm O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|}{d}w\Big)\Big)

(B.c.
|D_{2,t}|\geq\xi|D_{4,t}|
and
\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}\lesssim\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}
by
3.2
)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})\Big((1\pm O(\xi))\cdot 2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|w\pm O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|}{d}w\Big)\Big)

(By
Equation

B.145
)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})\cdot(1\pm O(\xi))\cdot 2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|w

(B.c.
\xi\geq\frac{1}{d}
)

In particular, for
w\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]
,

\displaystyle v_{t}(w)

\displaystyle\lesssim-(1-(1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2})^{2})\cdot(1-O(\xi))\cdot 2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|\gamma_{2}^{3/4}

(B.148)

\displaystyle\lesssim-(1-(1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2})^{2})\cdot\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|\gamma_{2}^{3/4}

(B.c.
\xi=\frac{1}{\log\log d}
)

\displaystyle\lesssim-(2\gamma_{2}^{1/2}-\gamma_{2})\cdot\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|\gamma_{2}^{3/4}

(B.149)

\displaystyle\lesssim-\gamma_{2}^{5/4}\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|

(B.c.
\gamma_{2}^{1/2}\geq\gamma_{2}
since
\gamma_{2}\leq 1
)

as desired.
∎

The next lemma essentially implies that if at some point
D_{4,t}\leq 0
and
D_{2,t}>0
, then it will never be the case that
D_{4,t}>0
and
D_{2,t}>0
.

Lemma B.17
.

Suppose we are in the setting of
Theorem

3.3
, and suppose
D_{4,t}=0
and
D_{2,t}\geq 0
for some
t\geq 0
. Then,
\frac{d}{ds}D_{4,s}\Big|_{s=t}\leq 0
.

Proof.

We can calculate that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}D_{4,t}=\frac{d}{dt}\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[{P_{4,d}}(w)]=\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[{P_{4,d}}^{\prime}(w)\cdot v_{t}(w)]

(B.150)

Recall from
Equation

A.4
that

\displaystyle{P_{4,d}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{2}+6d+8}{d^{2}-1}t^{4}-\frac{6d+12}{d^{2}-1}t^{2}+\frac{3}{d^{2}-1}

(B.151)

meaning that

\displaystyle{P_{4,d}}^{\prime}(t)=4t^{3}\pm O\Big(\frac{1}{d}\Big)\cdot(|t^{3}|+|t|)=4t^{3}\pm O\Big(\frac{1}{d}\Big)|t|

(B.152)

Observe that for
t\gtrsim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}
,
{P_{4,d}}^{\prime}(t)>0
and for
t\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}
, it may be the case that
{P_{4,d}}^{\prime}(t)<0
. In particular,

\displaystyle\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[{P_{4,d}}^{\prime}(w)\cdot v_{t}(w)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(|w|\lesssim 1/\sqrt{d})\cdot\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[{P_{4,d}}^{\prime}(w)\cdot v_{t}(w)\mid|w|\lesssim 1/\sqrt{d}]

(B.153)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\mathbb{P}(|w|\gtrsim 1/\sqrt{d})\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[{P_{4,d}}^{\prime}(w)\cdot v_{t}(w)\mid|w|\gtrsim 1/\sqrt{d}]

(B.154)

\displaystyle\leq O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|}{d^{2}}\Big)+\mathbb{P}(|w|\gtrsim 1/\sqrt{d})\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[{P_{4,d}}^{\prime}(w)\cdot v_{t}(w)\mid|w|\gtrsim 1/\sqrt{d}]

(B.c.
|w|\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}
and by
Lemma

B.16
)

\displaystyle\leq O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|}{d^{2}}\Big)+\mathbb{P}(|w|\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}])

(B.155)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[{P_{4,d}}^{\prime}(w)\cdot v_{t}(w)\mid|w|\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]]

(B.156)

\displaystyle\leq O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|}{d^{2}}\Big)-\mathbb{P}(|w|\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}])\gamma_{2}^{9/4}\cdot\gamma_{2}^{5/4}\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|

(B.c.
{P_{4,d}}^{\prime}(w)\gtrsim\gamma_{2}^{9/4}
, and by
Lemma

B.16
)

\displaystyle\leq O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|}{d^{2}}\Big)-\frac{\gamma_{2}}{2}\cdot\gamma_{2}^{14/4}\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|

(By
Lemma

B.15
)

\displaystyle<0

(By
3.2
since
d
is sufficiently large)

meaning that
\frac{d}{dt}D_{4,t}<0
, as desired.
∎

The next lemma essentially implies that if at some point
D_{4,t}\leq 0
and
D_{2,t}>0
, it will never be the case that
D_{4,t}\leq 0
and
D_{2,t}<0
.

Lemma B.18
.

Suppose we are in the setting of
Theorem

3.3
, and suppose
D_{4,t}\leq 0
and
D_{2,t}=0
, for some
t\geq 0
. Then,
\frac{d}{ds}D_{2,s}\Big|_{s=t}\geq 0
.

Proof.

Recall from
Equation

A.4
that
{P_{2,d}}(t)=\frac{d}{d-1}t^{2}-\frac{1}{d-1}
meaning that
{P_{2,d}}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{2d}{d-1}t
. Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}D_{2,t}=\frac{d}{dt}\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[w^{2}]=\frac{d}{d-1}\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[2w\cdot v_{t}(w)]

(B.157)

If
D_{4,t}\leq 0
and
D_{2,t}=0
, then for any particle
w\in[0,1]
, we can write

\displaystyle v_{t}(w)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})(P_{t}(w)+Q_{t}(w))

(By
Lemma

4.4
)

\displaystyle=-(1-w^{2})\Big(4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w^{3}\pm O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|}{d}\Big)|w|\Big)

(By
Lemma

4.4
)

\displaystyle=(1-w^{2})\Big(4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|w^{3}\pm O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|}{d}\Big)|w|\Big)

(B.158)

where the last equality is because
D_{4,t}\leq 0
. Observe that
v_{t}(w)\geq 0
for
w\gtrsim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}
, while it may be the case that
v_{t}(w)\leq 0
for
w\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[2w\cdot v_{t}(w)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(|w|\lesssim 1/\sqrt{d})\cdot\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[2w\cdot v_{t}(w)\mid|w|\lesssim 1/\sqrt{d}]

(B.159)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\mathbb{P}(|w|\gtrsim 1/\sqrt{d})\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[2w\cdot v_{t}(w)\mid|w|\lesssim 1/\sqrt{d}]

(B.160)

\displaystyle\geq-O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|}{d^{2}}\Big)+\mathbb{P}(|w|\gtrsim 1/\sqrt{d})\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[2w\cdot v_{t}(w)\mid|w|\gtrsim 1/\sqrt{d}]

(By bounding
Equation

B.158
when
|w|\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}
)

\displaystyle\geq-O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|}{d^{2}}\Big)+\mathbb{P}(|w|\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}])

(B.161)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[2w\cdot v_{t}(w)\mid|w|\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]]

(B.162)

\displaystyle\gtrsim-O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|}{d^{2}}\Big)+\mathbb{P}(|w|\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}])\cdot\gamma_{2}^{9/4}\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|

(By
Equation

B.158
)

\displaystyle\gtrsim-O\Big(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|}{d^{2}}\Big)-\frac{\gamma_{2}}{2}\cdot\gamma_{2}^{9/4}\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|

(By
Lemma

B.15
)

\displaystyle\geq 0

(By
3.2
since
d
is sufficiently large)

as desired.
∎

Putting all of the previous lemmas together, we obtain the following invariant: a lower bound for
|\Phi(w)-\Phi(w^{\prime})|
, for a large fraction of
w,w^{\prime}
, which holds throughout Phase 3, Case 1. Note that in the proof of this invariant, we need to make use of the fact that once we enter the case where
D_{2,t}>0
and
D_{4,t}<0
, we cannot leave this case. If this is not true, then we cannot use the fact that
|\Phi(w_{t})-\Phi(w_{t}^{\prime})|
is increasing as a function of
t
(
Lemma

B.11
) since if
D_{4,t}>0
at any point, then
|\Phi(w_{t})-\Phi(w_{t}^{\prime})|
could decrease, and we would not have control over this decrease.

Lemma B.19

(Phase 3, Case 1 Invariant)
.

Suppose we are in the setting of
Theorem

3.3
. Let
{T_{\textup{R}}}
be as defined in the statement of
Lemma

B.6
, and assume that
D_{2,T_{2}}=0
and
D_{4,T_{2}}<0
. Then, for all
t\geq{T_{\textup{R}}}
(in particular, for all times
t
during Phase 3 Case 1), if
\iota,\iota^{\prime}
are sampled independently from
\rho_{0}
,

\displaystyle|\Phi(w_{t}(\iota))-\Phi(w_{t}(\iota^{\prime}))|

\displaystyle\gtrsim\xi\kappa^{3}

(B.163)

with probability at least
1-O(\kappa)
.

Proof of
Lemma

B.19
.

By
Lemma

B.17
and
Lemma

B.18
, if
D_{2,T_{2}}=0
and
D_{4,T_{2}}<0
, then for all
t\geq T_{2}
, we will have
D_{2,t}\geq 0
and
D_{4,t}\leq 0
. Thus, the lemma follows from
Lemma

B.11
and
Lemma

B.14
, as well as the fact that
D_{4,t}\leq 0
for
t\in[{T_{\textup{R}}},T_{2}]
(meaning that the potential difference is increasing in the time interval
[{T_{\textup{R}}},T_{2}]
).
∎

Finally, we show
Lemma

4.10
which gives a running time bound for Phase 3, Case 1.

Proof of
Lemma

4.10
.

Suppose
t\geq T_{2}
and assume that
D_{2,T_{2}}=0
and
D_{4,t}<0
(i.e. Phase 3, Case 1 occurs). First, suppose
|D_{4,t}|\leq\xi|D_{2,t}|
where
\xi=\frac{1}{\log\log d}
. Then, by
Lemma

B.16
, if
|w|\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]
, we have

\displaystyle|v_{t}(w)|

\displaystyle\gtrsim\gamma_{2}^{5/4}\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|

(B.164)

(note that the lemma is stated for
w\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/4}]
, but holds for
|w|\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/4}]
since
v_{t}(w)
is an odd function). Therefore,

\displaystyle\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}|v_{t}(w)|^{2}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\rho_{t}}(|w|\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}])\cdot\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[|v_{t}(w)|^{2}\mid|w|\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]]

(B.165)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{\gamma_{2}}{2}\cdot\E_{w\sim\rho_{t}}[|v_{t}(w)|^{2}\mid|w|\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]]

(By
Lemma

B.15
)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{\gamma_{2}}{2}\cdot\gamma_{2}^{5/2}\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{4}|D_{2,t}|^{2}

(By
Equation

B.164
)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\gamma_{2}^{7/2}\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{4}|D_{2,t}|^{2}

(B.166)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\gamma_{2}^{7/2}\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}\cdot(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}|D_{2,t}|^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}|D_{4,t}|^{2})

(B.c.
\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}\lesssim\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}
(
3.2
) and
|D_{4,t}|\leq\xi|D_{2,t}|
)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\gamma_{2}^{7/2}\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}L(\rho_{t})

(B.167)

Thus, by
Lemma

B.35
, for any time
t
during Phase 3, Case 1 such that
|D_{4,t}|\leq\xi|D_{2,t}|
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{dL(\rho_{t})}{dt}

\displaystyle\lesssim-\gamma_{2}^{7/2}\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}L(\rho_{t})

(B.168)

Let
T_{*,\epsilon}
be as in
Theorem

3.3
, and define

\displaystyle I_{|D_{4}|\leq\xi|D_{2}|}=\{t\leq T_{*,\epsilon}\text{ and }t\text{ in Phase 3, Case 1}\mid|D_{4,t}|\leq\xi|D_{2,t}|\}

(B.169)

Then, by
Equation

B.168
,

\displaystyle\frac{dL(\rho_{t})}{dt}

\displaystyle\lesssim-\gamma_{2}^{7/2}\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}L(\rho_{t})1(t\in I_{|D_{4}|\leq\xi|D_{2}|})

(B.170)

Thus, by Gronwall’s inequality (
G.13
),

\displaystyle\frac{L(\rho_{T_{*,\epsilon}})}{L(\rho_{0})}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Big(-C\int_{T_{2}}^{T_{*,\epsilon}}\gamma_{2}^{7/2}\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}1(t\in I_{|D_{4}|\leq\xi|D_{2}|})dt\Big)

(B.171)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-C\gamma_{2}^{7/2}\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}\int_{I_{|D_{4}|\leq\xi|D_{2}|}}1dt\Big)

(B.172)

Since
L(\rho_{0})\lesssim(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})\gamma_{2}^{2}
by
Lemma

B.7
and
L(\rho_{T_{*,\epsilon}})\gtrsim(\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2})\epsilon^{2}
, rearranging gives

\displaystyle\int_{I_{|D_{4}|\leq\xi|D_{2}|}}1dt\lesssim\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}\gamma_{2}^{7/2}}\log\Big(\frac{\gamma_{2}^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}\Big)

(B.173)

Now, suppose
t
is such that
|D_{4,t}|\geq\xi|D_{2,t}|
. Let us lower bound the average velocity for
w\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/4}]
first (then, we can conclude the average velocity is large by
Lemma

B.15
). By
Lemma

4.4
,

\displaystyle|v_{t}(w)|

\displaystyle=(1-w^{2})|P_{t}(w)+Q_{t}(w)|

(B.174)

and we can write

\displaystyle P_{t}(w)=2\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}w+4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w^{3}=4\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}w(w-r)(w+r)

(B.175)

where
r=\sqrt{-\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{2,d}^{2}D_{2,t}}{2\hat{\sigma}_{4,d}^{2}D_{4,t}}}
is the positive root of
P_{t}
. For
|w|\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]
, we have

\displaystyle 1-w^{2}\geq 1-(1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2})^{2}=2\gamma_{2}^{1/2}-\gamma_{2}\geq\gamma_{2}^{1/2}.

(B.176)

Additionally,
|w|\geq\gamma_{2}^{3/4}
and
|w+r|\geq\gamma_{2}^{3/4}
. Thus, for
w\in[\gamma_{2}^{3/4},1-\gamma_{2}^{1/2}]
, we have

\displaystyle|v_{t}(w)|^{2}

\displaystyle\gtrsim(1-w^{2})^{2}\Big(P_{t}(w)+Q_{t}(w)\Big)^{2}

(B.177)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\gamma_{2}\Big(P_{t}(w)+Q_{t}(w)\Big)^{2}

(B.c.
1-w^{2}\geq\gamma_{2}^{1/2}
)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\gamma_{2}\cdot\Big(P_{t}(w)^{2}-2Q_{t}(w)^{2}\Big)

(B.c.
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
, so
a^{2}-2b^{2}\leq 2(a+b)^{2}
)
